le danger de la religion, mais le peuple
de = 219 1018 1138 · la = 583 382 · que = 197 194 · M. = 196 · vous = 569 · 13 = full stop

The Hague → Versailles · numerical word-and-syllable code of about 1,200 groups · January 1757 – January 1758

The comte d’Affry’s intercepted letters, 1757–58

Koninklijk Huisarchief, The Hague, Prins Willem V, inv. 192: code letters of the French ambassador to the foreign secretaries Rouillé and Bernis, opened by the Dutch post and copied for the stadholder’s cabinet. DECODE lists ten of them, all in one code, as not decrypted: no decipherment was imaged with them. Clear copies of all ten turned out to be in the Fagel registers of the Nationaal Archief (found by WAINUT after the decryption).

The Dutch codebreaker Pierre Lyonet’s decipherments of eight sibling letters are among the images, filed as ordinary clear pages. The key was recovered by aligning them group by group with their cipher, and applied to the letters with no decipherment on DECODE; 89% of the groups decrypted as sense from that key alone, 94% after values checked against clear copies found later in the Fagel registers.

Daniel Bourdeau · posted 19 September 2026 · updated 27 October 2026

Method: key recovered based on adjacent plaintext · Extent: partial (94% of the cipher)

Credits · WAINUT (pointer to a source) · Contemporary key or decipherment (key or decipherment used) · Th. Bussemaker (key or decipherment used)

adjacent text
On this pageThe documentsLyonet’s decipherments among the imagesThe keyThe decrypted lettersWhat remains openFull textsSources

At a glanceKey from adjacent plaintext · partial (94% of the cipher)

The letters
Eleven letters of d’Affry, Louis XV’s envoy at The Hague, 1757–58, to Rouillé and then to Bernis, intercepted by the Dutch.
Where
Koninklijk Huisarchief, The Hague, Prins Willem V inv. 192. DECODE lists them as not decrypted, saying the solutions were on separate sheets.
The cipher
One numerical code for the whole year; the key has 651 groups.
How it was read
Eight clear pages that look like d’Affry’s own letters are Lyonet’s decipherments of the cipher beside them. Aligned group by group (7 letters, about 3,000 groups) they give the key; nine letters then decrypt at 60–95% each. Checked against Bussemaker (1906) and Coquelle (1904).
What it says
The navy and the convoys, the Gouvernante’s tears, Ostend and Nieuport, spies sent to England, a French loan tried in London.
Still open
R1071 is not decrypted and R1072 gives about 78%. The letter to Stainville in Vienna is in another code. Lyonet’s own copies are in the Nationaal Archief, not digitised.

01 The documents

Louis-Auguste d’Affry (1713–1793), a Swiss officer in French service, was Louis XV’s envoy and then ambassador at The Hague from 1755 to 1762. His despatches went by the Dutch post, and the stadholder’s cabinet had them opened, copied and given to Pierre Lyonet (1706–1789), the naturalist who was also the Republic’s cipher secretary. Karl de Leeuw’s history of the Dutch black chamber (2000) records that Lyonet broke d’Affry’s first code in June 1756 and then “two more French ones”. The decipherments were copied for the Princess Governess and the regents. One set is in the Nationaal Archief (Stadhouderlijke Secretarie, 1.01.50 inv. 221 and 223, “grouped by the cipher used”, not digitised). Robert Fruin excerpted it in the 1850s, and Bussemaker printed his excerpts in 1906, as Dutch summaries with some verbatim French sentences.

Another set is inv. 192 of the Prins Willem V archive in the Koninklijk Huisarchief, catalogued on DECODE as nineteen records, R1052–R1076 and R2067. Each record is one letter: the intercept copy of the cipher, with the clear passages of d’Affry’s original written in. Seven are marked “solved by Dutch codebreakers, the solution written on separate sheets”, and eleven as not decrypted. Ten of the eleven are this target; the eleventh, R1071, is in another code and has its own page (d’Affry to Stainville, August 1757):

DECODEDate, numberToGroups
R10726 Jan 1757, no. 123Rouillé556
R10767 Jan 1757, no. 124Rouillé424
R107325 Jan 1757, no. 133Rouillé315
R10641 Feb 1757, no. 136Rouillé1,249
R10653 Feb 1757, no. 137Rouillé1,082 (has a decipherment; see below)
R10671 Apr 1757, no. 161Rouillé1,481
R106826 Apr 1757, no. 172Rouillé60
R105419 Jul 1757“B.” (Bernis; DECODE reads Bonnac)369
R107022 Jul 1757, no. 210“B.”400
R1074, R206717 Jan 1758, no. 283“B.” / d’Usson538 + two pages

02 Lyonet’s decipherments among the images

A first pass took the DECODE note literally and treated the siblings’ solutions as lost. The attack started from cribs: Bussemaker’s verbatim sentence of 19 July placed “des précautions” (800 698) in R1054 by its repeat twelve words apart, and “le danger de la religion” turned up in the 22 July letter. That showed a word-and-syllable nomenclator, but cribs and a language-model solver got no further than a few dozen groups.

The clear pages among the images are Lyonet’s decipherments. On R1066 (17 February) the clear page 5578, headed “N° 142 … D’A. à R.” with a clerk’s mark, begins with the same sentence as the intercept (“Mon courrier n’est arrivé que le 15 au soir”). It then goes on, where the intercept switches to figures, with “J’ai vu Mr de Slingerland chez moi ce matin”. It is Lyonet’s decipherment of the whole letter. The same mark heads clear copies in R1052, R1053, R1062, R1063, R1065, R1069 and R1075. Their openings fit their ciphers group for group: J’ai l’honneur de vous… = 865 1028 875 1018 569.

R1065, listed as not decrypted, is one of them. Its cipher is cut into thirteen numbered paragraphs that alternate between two sets of pages, and its clear copy is on four others. DECODE had noted a clear letter “whose relation to the ciphered periods is unclear”.

03 The key

The clear copies were transcribed from the scans and aligned with their ciphers one group at a time, each group graded H (forced by the text on both sides or repeated) or M. The seven letters were R1052, R1053, R1062, R1063, R1065, R1066 and R1069, plus the ciphered parts of R1075, about 3,300 groups. The alignments were made letter by letter and cross-checked; a vote over all of them gives the key. It is a two-part code: the numbers are not in alphabetical order. It has about 1,200 groups for letters, syllables, words and names, with homophones for the common ones (de 219, 1018, 1138; la 583, 382, 10; que 197, 194, 31). Some examples: 13 is a full stop, and 105, 107 and 797 are usually nulls. Leurs Hautes Puissances is 399 627 261. Le Roi de Prusse is 16, Hollande 294, Reischach 488, Sa Majesté 22, religion 578. The Jan–Feb and Apr–Jul letters differ in their habits (which homophones the clerk favoured) but not in the code.

The DECODE transcriptions needed correcting on the way. Two copyists wrote 8 as a looped figure that the transcribers read as 0, so 056 is really 856 and 099 is 899. This affects R1064, R1067, R1072 and R1075. Elsewhere groups are run together (58291 = 582 91) or lines dropped or repeated.

04 The decrypted letters

Each letter was decrypted with the key, and the few groups missing from the key were inferred from context and recorded with their evidence. Inferred values are in braces in the working files. The full French decryptions are in the repository; extracts follow.

6 January 1757 (R1072, no. 123): the Italian

The whole letter concerns “the Italian”, a spy who wants to be paid for earlier warnings of English designs on the French possessions in the two Indies. For a hundred louis and a pension he offers to leave at once for England and report on the English forces by land and sea, the secrets of the London cabinet, their intrigues in foreign courts, the plans for the 1757 campaign, and England’s correspondents in France. D’Affry thinks the money well spent if the man is sound. This is the weakest decryption (about two thirds in sense), and Bussemaker has no entry for this date. The Italian reappears on 3 February, when d’Affry pays him the hundred louis and he leaves for Helvoet.

7 January 1757 (R1076, no. 124): convoys and herring

A convoy the Dutch feared for had arrived: four ships were to escort it, and the storm kept two of them in port. D’Affry expects the navy to be enlarged sans augmentation dans les troupes de terre, and he expects the case to be pressed harder now that people see the English respect Dutch shipping only as far as it is protected. So every convoy through the Channel must be strongly escorted. In Amsterdam he talked with a regent about the herring. Bussemaker’s summary (no. 56): enlarge the navy without the army, protect trade, herring privileges for the republicans.

25 January 1757 (R1073, no. 133): a false rumour

It was said that d’Affry had given the States-General a memorial calling any increase of the land forces a breach of neutrality. The rumour was quashed, but he went to see the Grand Pensionary. He told him the King neither wished to meddle nor ever had, but as a friend would regret seeing the Republic take on heavy expenses. Steyn answered that the increase would meet many difficulties. If its opponents could not stop it, he said, they could at least get a declaration made first to Versailles, Vienna and London, by which the Republic would confirm its resolution to stay strictly neutral; nothing would come of it before about the twentieth of next month. The register copy (scan 42R) gives these words exactly, and corrects the key-only reading in about ten places: à cette occasion for à cet égard, avec le même froid for avec le même succès, vers le vingt du Mois, Il faudroit voir si celà ne va pas mieux.

1 February 1757 (R1064, no. 136): taxes and herring

The States of Holland had adjourned until the 20th, when the augmentation comes up. The war funds were no larger than usual, and the means proposed (a sixth added to the house tax, a general rise in excises, interest on public funds cut to 2%) were so heavy that the augmentation looked unlikely. D’Affry then argues for a temporary favour to the Dutch herring fishery while France lacks sailors. It could be revoked when no longer deserved, and it would widen the quarrel between the Republic and England. He asks for it only if Holland blocks the augmentation. Then comes intelligence on suspects travelling through Antwerp, Breda and Liège. Bussemaker’s no. 59 gives the first part point by point and leaves out the agents, as Fruin said he did.

1 April 1757 (R1067, no. 161): the Gouvernante

The Maastricht convoy crisis. D’Affry found the Pensionary “fort embarrassé”: ce qui se passe ici aujourd’hui n’est […] que pour le perdre. The same afternoon he read the King’s declaration to the Princess Governess:

«… tu n’es pas ministre; faut-il que ce soit moi qui favorise les moyens de faire du mal à mon père?» Je lui ai répondu que S.A.R. savoit jusqu’à quel point le Roi mon maître a cherché à éviter la guerre … Je demande à S.A.R. d’employer non seulement ses bons offices, mais son crédit et son pouvoir pour déterminer une réponse prompte et telle que le Roi a droit de l’espérer.

Coquelle quoted the Governess’s words in 1904 from the Paris original, with the day given as 5 April. The piece that Bussemaker’s no. 67 quotes about Wassenaer (“son fils cadet a actuellement un employ en Frise, dont la nomination dépend de Mme la Gouvernante”) is piece no. 7 of this letter. The numbered pieces follow the page order, not the number order.

26 April 1757 (R1068, no. 172)

Il a passé Vendredi au soir un courrier de Vienne à Londres. Vous savez mieux que moi ce dont il est chargé. M. de Colloredo doit faire passer à M. Starrenberg ce qu’on lui aura répondu. The key alone gave hier and read the name as d’Aremberg; the Fagel register copy (below) has Vendredi and Starrenberg, Starhemberg, the imperial ambassador at Versailles. The DECODE note asked whether this note was left undecrypted because a different code was used. It was not: it is the same code as no. 171, which Lyonet deciphered.

19 July 1757 (R1054, to Bernis)

Nobody knows the purpose of the Gouvernante’s journey to The Hague. If it is to push an augmentation of the land forces, d’Affry asks for orders at once:

… il me paroit qu’il seroit essentiel aux intérêts du Roi de m’envoyer des ordres sur le champ [de demander] une confirmation de tout ce que les Etats-Généraux ont promis à Sa Majesté, et en même tems une déclaration de leur part qu’ils ne feront camper aucun corps sur leurs frontières. Je sens la dureté de cette proposition …

The passage runs from group 145 to group 351, and Bussemaker’s French agrees with it word for word except in four places. The cipher has “M. le maréchal d’Estrées” where Fruin wrote “M. le Cte”. It has “de sa communication” for “de la communication du Royaume”. It spells out Leurs Hautes Puissances, and it has “des ordres” for “ordre de demander”. The page ends with an attack of gout.

22 July 1757 (R1070, no. 210, to Bernis): Ostend

The Gouvernante is said to be going to the States of Holland to speak for her father George II, pointing to the danger to the Protestant religion. D’Affry doubts she will: if she proposes land troops, the other side will propose ships. Her party protested at the French occupation of Ostend and Nieuport. D’Affry notes that sensible people see the other side of it: with Ostend closed to the English, Amsterdam and Rotterdam will take over the trade England did with the Low Countries. Then Bussemaker’s sentence, group for group: Il y a quelques villes où quelques ministres hollandois ont prêché sur le danger de la religion, mais le peuple n’en paroit pas animé. If the Princess takes the Ostend affair as a pretext, he will ask her for an audience without waiting for orders.

17 January 1758 (R1074, no. 283, and R2067)

The first part is an extract from a correspondent’s letter. A French loan of two millions had been tried in England by “des gens si maladroits” that the ministry heard of it and a new law followed. It could still be got round: four millions at four or four and a half per cent at most, given “un profond secret, une parfaite connoissance du pays”. D’Affry adds that a Sieur Mac[donald] of the Bulkeley regiment has passed through on his way from France to England, and he suspects him of carrying commissions for the enemy. Some Scots and Irish in French service are spies, he writes, but to act would throw suspicion on the rest. The companion letter of the same day to d’Usson (R2067) has no DECODE transcription. It was transcribed from the images and decrypted at about 91%. It is d’Affry’s own letter of the same number, covering the extract. He thinks money operations in England useless: if found out, they would make France’s needs look greater than they are and put the English on their guard. Englishmen will still lend in France when the interest is right, and some already hold French life annuities. He adds that two or three Englishmen are at The Hague, one of them Mac…, and that two Spaniards from Paris, one a secretary of the Spanish embassy, are on their way to London.

05 What remains open

  • Re-measured 5 October 2026. The 21 September figure (86%) counted every group that had a value. A sense measure (measure_sense.py) now counts a group only if its nine-group window reads as French: 89.0% of the 9,306 groups in this code (Lyonet’s eight letters counted as read; rerun 14 October 2026 without R1071, which is in another code and was counted unread until then, giving 82.3%). Four letters were re-transcribed from the images, which recovered the copyist’s looped 8 that DECODE reads as 0 and 135 groups the earlier parse had lost: R1072 0.66 → 0.89, R1067 0.66 → 0.79, R1074 0.70 → 0.75, R1064 0.77 → 0.81. Pooling every occurrence fixed eighteen more code values (lui, dit, faire, Écosse, la Gouvernante …).
  • R1071 (4 August 1757, to Stainville in Vienna) is in a different code: its own page.
  • Per letter, 5–12% of groups stay open (listed as [n] in each decryption file). R1072 gives only about two thirds in sense: its copy is the worst transcribed.
  • Grades: values forced by a decipherment are H; values inferred from context in the letters without a decipherment are M or ? and listed with their evidence in read/R*_new.tsv. 56 groups of the key are ambiguous between two values (amb_M.json).
  • The eight decipherments themselves are Lyonet’s. The contribution here is the key and the decryption of the nine letters that have none on DECODE.
Contributed, 21 October 2026. WAINUT, a Dutch AI company, found that the clear text of all ten letters without a Lyonet sheet is digitised after all: clerks’ copies in the Fagel registers, Nationaal Archief 1.10.29 inv. 5186 (1757) and 5187 (1758), in date order with number and addressee (pull request #65). Aligned against them, this key gives the register’s word for 4,333 of 5,554 keyed groups (78%), against at most 21% for shuffled keys; the per-letter figures were re-run here and reproduce. The registers were found after the decryption and were not used for it: the 89% is a reading from the key alone. They settle open groups (projets, lundi, mardi, là dessus …), contradict some key values (675 is vingt, not deux; 1106 froid, not succès; 992 is -er, not des, at all eleven places) and correct the 25 January letter in about ten places.

Folded into the key, 21 October 2026. The proposals were checked on sixteen register scans read here, and each candidate was set against Lyonet’s own decipherments. 58 values were adopted: 21 groups that had no value and 37 values replaced (from the key or inferred earlier). Seven were dropped because Lyonet reads them otherwise as often or more: some groups really take two values (moi and mois, le roi de Prusse and prussien, utile and utilité). Re-measured, the ten letters with no contemporary decipherment on DECODE go from 82.4% to 86.0% of their groups read as sense, and the whole code from 89.0% to 91.3% (8,492 of 9,306); R1073 reaches 94%, R1072 93.5%, R1074 77.5%. Against the register text the key now matches 81% of keyed groups (was 78%); that figure is partly circular, since the new values come from the same registers.

Second pass, the same day. 131 groups still had no value. For 86 of them the register gives a clean word, seen on its scan (two more scans read, 63R and 248R) and not contradicted by Lyonet: Allemagne, Berlin, Wassenaer, question, six, toutes, possible, huit … The other 45 stay open: the register piece is a single letter or a syllable cut at the edge of the alignment, or the occurrences disagree. The code now reads at 94.1% (8,761 of 9,306 groups), and the ten letters with no decipherment on DECODE at 90.6%; R1067 went from 79% to 92%, R1074 from 75% to 89%. Still open: those 45 groups and the groups that take two values.

Checked on the images, 27 October 2026. The last five letters still measured on the DECODE transcription (R1054, R1068, R1070, R1073, R1076) were checked line by line on the scans. DECODE had dropped two whole lines of R1076 (the first lines of its paragraphs 4 and 8) and two groups of R1070, split three groups of R1054 at a superscript correction, and run groups together in R1073; R1070 and R1076 have their two pages in reverse order on DECODE. Measured on the new transcriptions the code reads at 94.2% (8,787 of 9,327 groups); R1073 rises from 93.9% to 96.2% and R1076 from 87.3% to 88.7%. A few groups lie in the binding or under ink.

The transcription and the decryption of every document on this page, so that the reading can be used and checked. Short files are shown here; all are in the repository.

Decryption

R1052.txt

Je ne peux me dispenser de vous faire part d'un bruit sourd qui s'est repandu ici, d'une pretendue prophetie faite par un homme habitant à Amsterdam, qui en disant à Mr Thomas Hop que le vaisseau dont il etoit en peine n'avoit point été pris par les Anglois; mais qu'il avoit peri à telle hauteur, ajoute, qu'il savoit aussi qu'on devoit attenter aux jours de Sa Majesté le 15 de ce mois. Je saurai dès demain si ces propos ont été veritablement tenus, et en ce cas je ne perdrai pas un instant à demander que cet insensé soit enfermé dans la maison des fous.
Translation

R1052_en.md

<!-- English translation of plain/R1052.txt (Claude Opus 5.5, 23 Oct 2026): Lyonet's contemporary clear decipherment of d'Affry's ciphered passage, imaged on the DECODE record R1052 and transcribed here. -->

# R1052, d'Affry, The Hague, 1757: English translation of the contemporary decipherment

I cannot refrain from informing you of a muffled rumour that has spread here, of a supposed prophecy made by a man
living in Amsterdam, who, while telling Mr Thomas Hop that the ship he was worried about had not been taken by the
English but had perished at such-and-such a latitude, added that he also knew that an attempt was to be made on His
Majesty's life on the 15th of this month. I shall know by tomorrow whether these words were really spoken, and in that
case I shall not lose an instant in asking that this madman be shut up in the madhouse.

R1053

Lyonet's contemporary clear decipherment, imaged on the DECODE record and transcribed here.

Decryption

R1053.txt

Il est arrivé hier ici un courrier de Mr de Haren, Ministre de la Republique à Bruxelles, par lequel ce Ministre donne avis de la marche de l'Armée du Roi, composée de Nonante Mille hommes. Cette nouvelle a dabord allarmé, et quelques Republicains sont venus me demander si je le savois. Je les ai assuré que non; et je leur ai dit que vous ne manqueriez pas de me mettre en état de notifier aux Ministres de la Republique la marche de cette armée, et de témoigner en même tems à Leurs Hautes Puissances la droiture des intentions de Sa Majesté à leur égard. J'ai ajouté que si le courrier que j'attendois m'en aportoit la nouvelle, j'en ferois part sur le champ, et que si vous ne me le marquiez pas on pouvoit compter que cette nouvelle n'étoit qu'un panneau du parti anglois pour alterer notre confiance mutuelle, mais j'esperois que l'on n'en seroit la duppe. Vous jugez, Mr, combien il est important que je sois instruit de bonne heure de tout ce que Sa Majesté jugera devoir être communiqué ici. Mr Burdin, qui m'a porté une lettre de vous de recommandation, s'est informé depuis Anvers jusqu'ici et dans les differentes routes qu'il a fait en Hollande d'un officier Suisse au service des Etats Generaux, nommé Ficher. Cet officier est parti il y a quelques jours pour Paris accompagné d'un François arrivant d'Angleterre, qui doit avoir emprunté beaucoup d'argent à Amsterdam de la part de l'Angleterre pour faire à Paris et dans le Royaume le metier d'espion. Ce François s'appelle Meunier; mais il a traduit son nom en Anglois, et doit porter celui de Muller ou de Miller. Il y a aparence que Ficher cherchera à voir Mr Burdin par lequel on pourroit decouvrir la verité de ce que j'ai eu l'honneur de vous marquer.
Translation

R1053_en.md

<!-- English translation of plain/R1053.txt (Claude Opus 5.5, 23 Oct 2026): Lyonet's contemporary clear decipherment, imaged on the DECODE record R1053 and transcribed here. -->

# R1053, d'Affry, The Hague, 1757: English translation of the contemporary decipherment

A courier arrived here yesterday from Mr de Haren, the Republic's Minister at Brussels, by which this Minister gives
notice of the march of the King's army, made up of ninety thousand men. This news at first caused alarm, and some
Republicans came to ask me whether I knew of it. I assured them that I did not; and I told them that you would not
fail to put me in a position to notify the Ministers of the Republic of the march of this army, and at the same time
to assure Their High Mightinesses of the uprightness of His Majesty's intentions towards them. I added that if the
courier I was expecting brought me the news, I would communicate it at once, and that if you did not tell me of it,
they could count on this news being nothing but a trap of the English party to undermine our mutual confidence; but I
hoped no one would be its dupe. You can judge, Sir, how important it is that I be informed early of everything His
Majesty sees fit to have communicated here. Mr Burdin, who brought me a letter of recommendation from you, has made
enquiries from Antwerp to here, and along the various routes he has travelled in Holland, about a Swiss officer in the
service of the States General named Ficher. This officer left a few days ago for Paris, accompanied by a Frenchman
arriving from England, who is said to have borrowed a great deal of money in Amsterdam on England's behalf in order to
ply the trade of spy in Paris and in the kingdom. This Frenchman is called Meunier; but he has translated his name
into English, and is said to go by that of Muller or Miller. It seems likely that Ficher will try to see Mr Burdin,
through whom one might discover the truth of what I have had the honour to tell you.

R1054, 19 Jul 1757

The purpose of the Gouvernante's (Princess Anne's) journey to The Hague is not known. Some think she comes to push an augmentation of the land forces; her (pro-Orange / anti-French) party and even the ministers are starting to raise alarm; others say she wants a naval augmentation.

# R1054 — d'Affry to Bernis ("B"), The Hague, 19 July 1757

DECODE R1054 (catalogue name gives d'Usson de Bonnac as addressee), image IMG_R1054_I5511_P1, page 1 only.
Clear heading "Du 19 Juillet 1757"; the whole page below it is code, 372 groups (the letter continues beyond this page).
Bussemaker (BMHG 27) no. 75, 19 Juillet 1757.

Read: 292 groups from key_M + 51 inferred = 343/372 (92%); 29 groups unresolved.
Inferred values in {braces} (listed in R1054_new.tsv); several replace wrong key_M values (792, 800, 66, 749, 193, 307).

qu'ils ne fe{ront} {ca}mper aucun corps sur leurs {fron}{tières}. Je {sens} la dureté de {cette} proposition, mais il me paroit

Un attaque de goutte m['a empêché?] de voir autant de monde que par le ... (continues on next page)

## Summary

The purpose of the Gouvernante's (Princess Anne's) journey to The Hague is not known. Some think she comes to push an
augmentation of the land forces; her (pro-Orange / anti-French) party and even the ministers are starting to raise alarm;
others say she wants a naval augmentation. If it is the land forces, d'Affry asks for orders to demand at once a
confirmation of the States-General's promises to the King and a declaration that they will camp no corps on their
frontiers; he justifies it by saying the King, trusting their word, did not order Marshal d'Estrées to secure his
communications, but would have to if the government were dragged by the English party into hostile measures.
He ends noting an attack of gout kept him from seeing people.

## Comparison with Bussemaker no. 75

The Dutch summary matches the first half. The verbatim passage matches the cipher group for group, with these differences:
- crib "m'envoyer ordre de demander sur le champ" — cipher "m'envoyer des ordres sur le champ" (992 151); no "demander"
  visible, though 166-168 (1136 338 371) are not fully split.
- crib "M. le Cte d'Estrées" — cipher "M. le maréchal d'Estrées" (6 185 885 1058 1084).
- crib "la sureté de la communication du Royaume" — cipher "de sa communication" (10 = sa) and no separate "du".
- crib "le Gouvernement étoit entrainé" — cipher 368 1186 1075 732 134; the split gou-vernement/étoit/en-traî-né is inferred.
- "L.H.P." is written out "leur hautes puissances" (399 627 261).
Otherwise Fruin/Bussemaker's quotation appears faithful.

## DECODE digit corrections

- pos 250: "26?" at the wrinkled right margin is 261 (puissances), as in pos 332.
- Transcriber's marks at rows 4, 20, 21 (8^6, 878^6, 7^140) not further checked; pos 44/45/273 read as transcribed.
- Right-margin truncation likely also at row ends marked "?" (pos 25 "4?", pos 38 "750?", pos 264 "75?", pos 278 "800?");
  75 read as "rendre" fits, the others unverified. No image crops were made.

# R1054 - d'Affry, The Hague, 19 July 1757 (to Bernis, "B."): reading

Rests on: the contemporary clear copy of this despatch in the Fagel register, Nationaal Archief 1.10.29 inv. 5186
scans 248R-249L (margin "Dat: 19. Juïllet 1757. A: à B:"), transcribed by WAINUT (21 Oct 2026,
`wainut/register_clear.txt`) and checked line by line against the NA IIIF scans 23 Oct 2026 (r2q). The register is the
deciphered text of the same letter: with this folder's key (`wainut/align_register.py`, `wainut/align/R1054.tsv`), 319
of the 364 keyed groups stand at the register's word (0.876; shuffled key values 0.144, max 0.206), from first group
to last. The DECODE record has one cipher page (image I5511, 372 groups); it ends inside the sentence on the gout
("... autant de monde que par le"), and the rest of the letter is not on the record. The reading covers that page; the
register's continuation is given below the reading. The reading follows the register in the clerk's spelling, except
where the figures give another word; those places are listed under "Figures over the register" and in
`R1054_reread_log.md`. Groups the register passes over without a word are listed in the log, not put into the reading.
The keyed decode this replaces is `R1054.md`.

## Reading

On ne dit point encore l'Objet du Voyage de Madame la Gouvernante: Les uns croyent, que c'est pour faire travailler à
une Augmentation dans les Troupes de Terre. Il est certain, que tous les Partisans murmurent sur l'état de foiblesse
dans lequel on laisse la Republique. Les Ministres commencent même à mettre en usage le danger de la Religion.
D'autres disent, que Son Altesse Royale, forcée par les plaintes reïtereés des Negociants veut proposer un Armement de
Mer. Il est à desirer que ce soit ce dernier objet qui l'amene ici. Si au contraire Elle travailloit à faire passer
une Augmentation dans les Troupes de Terre, il me paroit qu'il seroit essentiel aux Interets du Roy de m'envoyer ordre
de demander sur le Champ une confirmation de tout ce que les Etats Generaux ont promis à Sa Majesté, et en même tems
une Declaration de Leur part, qu'ils ne feront camper aucun Corps sur Leurs Frontieres. Je sens la dureté de cette
proposition, mais il me paroit qu'elle devient naturelle, en y ajoutant que Sa Majesté, par la juste confiance qu'Elle
avoit à la Parole de Leurs Hautes Puissances, n'a point ordonné à M. le Maréchal d'Estreés de prendre des precautions
pour la sureté de sa communication du Royaume, mais que des precautions deviendroient necessaires si malgré la Parole
donnée, le Gouvernement étoit entrainé par les Partisans de l'Angleterre, et forcé par l'insulte du Peuple à des
dispositions et à des demarches qui fissent craindre que Leurs Hautes Puissances ne s'écartassent malgré Elles de la
Sagesse de Leurs Regles. Une attaque de Goute m'empéche de voir autant de Monde que par le [...]

## Rest of the letter (register only, not on this record's image)

[...] que je le voudrois. J'espere malgé cela être au cour, et pouvoir vers la fin de la Semaine, ou aujourdhui en
huit vous en rendre compte. Je joins ici la Relation sur l'Objet de Delfzyl, elle m'a été remise hier fort tard. Vous
y verrez aussi les nouvelles d'Angleterre du 12. J'ai reçu aujourdhui une Lettre de Mr. de Moras du 11. qui me
confirme dans ce que j'ai eu l'honneur de vous marquer au Sujet de l'Oostfrise. Mr. le Cte. de Wassenaer, à qui j'en
ai parlé hier, pense comme moi. Il doit m'envoyer aujourdhui l'Abregé des Articles de la Convention du Roy
d'Angleterre, et de Mr. de Bentheim, pour la Comtesse de ce Nom. P. S. Le Comte de Wassenaer ne m'a point encore remis
l'Abregé dont j'ai eu l'honneur de vous parler. J'aurai celui de vous le faire tenir dès qu'il me sera parvenu.

## Figures over the register

- "n'a point ordonné à M. le Maréchal d'Estreés" for the register's "n'a point ordonné au Cte. d'Estreés": groups 402
  196 878 185 (à, m, le, maréchal; 185 = maréchal also in R1064 'M. le Marechal de Belle Isle'); DECODE transcribed
  '878^6', which the parse had split into 8 and 786; the register (and Bussemaker) have 'au Cte.' (d'Estrées was made
  maréchal de France in February 1757).
- "pour la sureté de sa communication du Royaume" for the register's "pour la sureté de la communication du Royaume":
  group 10 = sa (11 other occurrences at sa); 'du' has no group of its own and is the register's.
- "que par le [...]" for the register's "que je le voudrois.": the page ends with 197 306 628 (que, par, le); 306 =
  par at 34 other places; the register's 'que je le voudrois' has no group for 'je' or 'voudrois' here, and the letter
  goes on on a page that is not on the DECODE record.

<!-- English translation of read/R1054_reading.md (Claude Opus 5.5, 23 Oct 2026): the reading follows the contemporary clear copy in the Fagel register, aligned with the keyed decode; three places follow the figures. -->

# R1054, d'Affry to Bernis, The Hague, 19 July 1757: English translation

The purpose of Madame the Gouvernante's journey is still not being given out. Some believe it is to work for an
augmentation of the land forces. It is certain that all the partisans are grumbling about the state of weakness in
which the Republic is being left. The ministers are even beginning to make use of the danger to religion. Others say
that Her Royal Highness, pressed by the repeated complaints of the merchants, means to propose a naval armament. It is
to be wished that this last object is what brings her here. If on the contrary she were to work at getting an
augmentation of the land forces passed, it seems to me that it would be essential to the King's interests to send me
orders to ask at once for a confirmation of everything the States-General have promised His Majesty, and at the same
time a declaration on their part that they will not have any corps encamped on their frontiers. I feel the harshness
of this proposal, but it seems to me that it becomes natural if one adds that His Majesty, from the just confidence he
had in the word of Their High Mightinesses, has not ordered Marshal d'Estrées to take precautions for the security of
his communication with the kingdom, but that precautions would become necessary if, in spite of the word given, the
government were dragged by the partisans of England, and forced by the insults of the populace, into dispositions and
steps that made one fear that Their High Mightinesses were departing, against their will, from the wisdom of their
rules. An attack of gout keeps me from seeing as many people as [...]

(The cipher page ends here. The register continues: "... as I would like. I hope nevertheless to be in the know, and
to be able to report to you towards the end of the week or a week from today. I enclose the account of the Delfzijl
affair, which was handed to me late yesterday; you will also find in it the news from England of the 12th. I received
today a letter from M. de Moras of the 11th, which confirms me in what I had the honour to tell you about East
Friesland. The Count of Wassenaer, to whom I spoke of it yesterday, thinks as I do. He is to send me today the summary
of the articles of the convention between the King of England and M. de Bentheim, for the Countess of that name. P.S.
The Count of Wassenaer has not yet handed me the summary I had the honour to mention to you. I shall have that of
sending it to you as soon as it reaches me.")

Decryption

R1062.txt

L'Assemblée de Hollande est commencée. On doit y agiter la question de l'augmentation, et si les debats sont vifs je serai averti à tems pour parler sans m'écarter cependant de ce que vous me prescrivez. Je sais surement que le Corps combiné qui doit s'assembler pour couvrir Hanovre, et s'il se peut la Gueldres, devoit être commandé par Mr de Cumberland, comme j'ai eu l'honneur de vous le marquer; mais le Roi de Prusse ne le veut pas. On a proposé ce Commandement au Duc Louis Wolfenbuttel Feld Marechal de la Republique, qui l'a refusé. Je suis assuré de ceci, et c'est un grand secret. Il est aisé de juger qu'on esperoit engager moyennant cela plus facilement la Republique. Le Baron de Reischach vient de me dire qu'il savoit positivement que le Roi de Prusse auroit des subsides de l'Angleterre, et que si le Ministere Anglois ne pouvoit les lui faire accorder apresent par la Nation, ce seroit tacitement qu'il les auroit.
(Je crois que dans la Lettre de Mr de Hattel, il est dit que ce Chevau Leger est Breton. Le hazard m'a amené un Negociant de Lyon qui dit être parti d'Anvers pour Breda avec un homme qui en chemin lui dit qu'il etoit ancien Mousquetaire; qu'il entroit au service du Roi de Prusse; qu'il abandonnoit une femme jeune, quatre enfans, et beaucoup de bien, parce qu'il ne pouvoit plus retourner en France; et qu'il y auroit dans peu de grands changements dans le Royaume; qu'il etoit de Saint Brieux en Bretagne; qu'il avoit voulu faciliter aux Anglois une descente dans ce Païs là; et qu'il y avoit eu des intelligences. Cet homme a donné à ce Negociant des adresses de sa main que vous trouverez dans ce Paquet, et un itineraire pour le Negociant, au cas qu'il voulût aller en Bretagne, et que celui ci m'a donné. Il a ajouté que cet homme etoit à Cologne, et qu'il viendroit peut-être à Amsterdam. Leur conversation a été le vingt huit ____ dernier en chemin et à Breda. Cet homme s'est apellé à Bruxelles Mr Delf, et il doit avoir repris son Nom de Comte d'Urvoy. Dans le trouble où je suis, je n'ai point songé à faire faire une copie de la Lettre du Baron de Hatzel; mais comme vous l'avez, vous pourrez voir si malgré la difference d'etats, cet homme est le même, ou si ce sont deux Monstres. Le Negociant de Lyon nommé Santerre est revenu cet après diné chez moi, et m'a porté l'empreinte du Cachet du Comte d'Urvoy
Translation

R1062_en.md

<!-- English translation of plain/R1062.txt (Claude Opus 5.5, 23 Oct 2026): Lyonet's contemporary clear decipherment, imaged on the DECODE record R1062 and transcribed here. The blank "____" of the copy is kept. -->

# R1062, d'Affry, The Hague, 1757: English translation of the contemporary decipherment

The Assembly of Holland has begun. The question of the augmentation is to be debated there, and if the debates are
lively I shall be warned in time to speak, without however departing from what you prescribe to me. I know for certain
that the combined corps which is to assemble to cover Hanover, and if possible Guelders, was to be commanded by Mr de
Cumberland, as I had the honour to tell you; but the King of Prussia will not have it. The command was offered to Duke
Louis of Wolfenbüttel, Field Marshal of the Republic, who refused it. I am sure of this, and it is a great secret. It
is easy to judge that they hoped by this means to draw the Republic in more easily. Baron von Reischach has just told
me that he knew positively that the King of Prussia would have subsidies from England, and that if the English
Ministry could not have them granted to him by the Nation at present, he would have them tacitly.

(I believe that in Mr de Hattel's letter it is said that this light horseman is a Breton. Chance has brought me a
merchant from Lyon who says he left Antwerp for Breda with a man who told him on the way that he was a former
musketeer; that he was entering the service of the King of Prussia; that he was abandoning a young wife, four children
and a great deal of property, because he could no longer return to France; and that there would shortly be great
changes in the kingdom; that he was from Saint-Brieuc in Brittany; that he had wanted to make it easier for the English
to land in that country; and that there had been secret dealings. This man gave the merchant addresses in his own
hand, which you will find in this packet, and an itinerary for the merchant in case he wished to go to Brittany,
which the merchant gave to me. He added that this man was at Cologne and might perhaps come to Amsterdam. Their
conversation took place on the twenty-eighth of ____ last, on the road and at Breda. This man called himself Mr Delf at
Brussels, and is said to have taken back his name of Comte d'Urvoy. In the distress I am in, I did not think to have a
copy made of Baron von Hatzel's letter; but as you have it, you will be able to see whether, despite the difference of
condition, this man is the same, or whether they are two monsters. The merchant from Lyon, named Santerre, came back to
my house this afternoon and brought me the impression of the Comte d'Urvoy's seal

Decryption

R1063.txt

N° [134] D'A. à R. Du 27 Janvier 1757
J'écris aujourd'hui à Mr de Hatzel comme vous me l'ordonnez; mais je crois qu'il n'aura rien à nous reveler que de vague. Je suis persuadé que l'homme dont il nous parle n'est autre chose que le Comte d'Urvoy. On n'a point encore entendu parler de lui dans ce Païs. Les bruits de l'augmentation de Terre prennent vivement depuis avanthier au soir; à en croire le public elle va être determinée. J'ai lieu (de croire) qu'Elle ne le sera pas si tôt. Comme Mr de Reischach a la permission de sa Cour de presser plus ou moins la demande du secours, j'ai cru devoir Lui conseiller de voir les Ministres pour leur demander à quoi cette affaire en étoit. Cela a produit ce que j'en esperois; c'est à dire de l'embarras, et tout embarras est un moyen leur de retarder, et quelque fois même d'anéantir les Projets qui pourroient nous gêner. La Cour de Vienne a en main de quoi les retenir, par les questions qu'Elle peut agiter sur les Places de la Barriere, et sur tout sur l'occupation de Namur. Daignez vous rapeller ce que j'ai eu l'honneur de vous en dire; mais comme ce moyen est très delicat, je crois qu'il faut pour l'employer de concert avec Vienne, attendre que la delicatesse du moment nous y conduise; car il pourroit arriver l'evenement le plus singulier qui seroit que l'Imperatrice auroit malgré Elle une Guarnizon dans une de ces Places les plus fortes, que ces étrangers approvisionnent amplement de tout. Comme je ne suis instruit de rien, je ne puis que vous mettre sous les yeux une idée de ce qui (peut) arriver. Je ne puis croire que ce Païs manque de raison au point de vouloir s'engager et augmenter les troubles qui agitent l'Europe; mais si cela est, une explication prompte sur Namur seroit plus essentielle que je ne peux l'exprimer.
Translation

R1063_en.md

<!-- English translation of plain/R1063.txt (Claude Opus 5.5, 23 Oct 2026): Lyonet's contemporary clear decipherment, imaged on the DECODE record R1063 and transcribed here. Words in round brackets are the copy's own insertions. -->

# R1063, d'Affry to Rouillé, The Hague, 27 January 1757 (no. 134): English translation of the contemporary decipherment

No. [134], d'A[ffry] to R[ouillé], 27 January 1757.

I am writing today to Mr de Hatzel as you order me; but I believe he will have nothing but vague things to reveal to
us. I am persuaded that the man he speaks of to us is none other than the Comte d'Urvoy. Nothing has yet been heard of
him in this country. The rumours of the augmentation of the land forces have been gaining ground strongly since the
evening before yesterday; to believe the public, it is about to be decided. I have reason (to believe) that it will not
be so soon. As Mr von Reischach has his court's permission to press the request for help more or less, I thought I
should advise him to see the Ministers and ask them how matters stood. This produced what I hoped for, that is,
embarrassment; and every embarrassment is a means for them to delay, and sometimes even to destroy, the projects that
might hamper us. The court of Vienna has in hand the means of holding them back, through the questions it can raise
about the Barrier towns, and above all about the occupation of Namur. Be pleased to recall what I had the honour of
telling you about it; but as this means is very delicate, I believe that to use it in concert with Vienna we must wait
until the delicacy of the moment leads us to it; for the most singular thing might happen, namely that the Empress
would have, against her will, a garrison in one of the strongest of those towns, which these foreigners supply amply
with everything. As I am informed of nothing, I can only put before you an idea of what (may) happen. I cannot believe
that this country is so lacking in reason as to wish to commit itself and increase the troubles that agitate Europe;
but if it is so, a prompt explanation about Namur would be more essential than I can express.

R1064, 1 Feb 1757, no. 136

Decryption

R1064.md

# R1064 - d'Affry to Rouillé, The Hague, 1 February 1757

No. 136 ("N:136", "D'A. à R.", "Du 1 Février 1757" in clear at the head of p.1). Five pages, IMG_R1064_I5561..I5565
(P1-P5). **No clear copy (Lyonet decipherment) among the images**: the only clear text is the heading on 5561, the
paragraph labels, and a clear postscript at the foot of 5565 ("Je joins ici deux lettres une pour M. de Montera[?] et
l'autre pour M. de Crémille. Vous trouverez dans ce paquet 3 exemplaires d'un ouvrage auquel M. Rouillé d'Orfeuil a
fourni la forme et la diction ... La vérité des faits"). So this is a reading with the key, not an alignment; no
align/R1064.tsv was written.

Layout: the cipher is written in numbered paragraphs set out of order across the pages, as in R1065:
5561 = §1, §3 ("B:3"); 5562 = §5, §7, §9, §11; 5563 = §2 ("Pag2"), §4, §6, §8 (running onto the top of 5564);
5564 = §10, §12; 5565 = §13, then the clear postscript. Read in numerical order the joins are seamless
(§1 "vous rendre com|p te" §2; §11 "du sup|plément" §12; §12 "le comte de Ma|urepas" §13; §4 "le béné|fice" §5 ...).
Groups parsed afresh from decode/DOC_R1064_D1846_1846.txt (cipher_U.txt's R1064 line has run-together groups):
1249 groups, 1033 keyed (key_M) + 176 inferred (R1064_new.tsv) = 1209 (97%); about 40 unresolved. Many inferred
values are M/? and the reading of §§9-13 (names, passport list) is rough.

## English summary

The States of Holland have adjourned and reassemble on the 20th to deal with the army augmentation, which d'Affry
hopes will fail. The war funds for this year are no larger than last year's, and the proposed means (a sixth added
to the house tax, a general rise in the excises, and cutting interest on public funds to 2%) are so burdensome that
the States are unlikely to adopt them. On herring: he has learnt how important it is to reduce the Dutch fishery's
profit and encourage the French one, but with French sailors short in wartime he thinks a temporary favour to the
Dutch fishery would cost little, would be revocable if the Republic misbehaved, would please nearly all the Dutch,
and would widen the Anglo-Dutch quarrel over the fishery; he would ask for it only on condition that Holland resists
the augmentation. The rest is intelligence: a suspect (Charles de Quintin, travelling from Antwerp to Breda with a
Lyon merchant, flagged by M. de Hatzel) will be arrested if he comes to Holland; a captain of the Löwendal regiment
has arrived; "the Italian" reports that one La Combe (translator of the Lettres philosophiques) and an old man from
Liège have left England for France to report on the French navy; and a Paris article in the Amsterdam gazette
supplement about two ship captains is answered with an extract of d'Affry's passport register (captains of the
Comte de Maurepas and another Bordeaux ship, and a surgeon from Coutras).

## Comparison with Bussemaker no. 59 (1 Février 1757)

Bussemaker: States of Holland adjourned until the 20th, then the augmentation comes up; war funds no more than usual
and the means so oppressive that the augmentation becomes unlikely: a sixth surcharge on the house tax, a general
increase of the excises, reduction of interest on public funds to 2%; France should protect its own herring fishery
against Dutch competition, but not at present, with French sailors lacking, so favouring the Dutch would do no harm;
the favour is temporary and can stop once the Republic no longer deserves it; it would please the Dutch most and,
herring being a bone of contention with England, estrange the two countries; he asks for it only if Holland blocks
the troop augmentation. This matches §§1-8 point by point and fixes 674 = 20, 981 = x (sixième), 42 = générale.
Bussemaker (Fruin) omits the intelligence paragraphs §§3-4 and §§9-13, as his preface says he did for agents and
intriguers.
Reading

R1064_reading.md

# R1064 - d'Affry, The Hague, 1 February 1757 (no. 136, to Rouillé): reading

Rests on: the contemporary clear copy of this despatch in the Fagel register, Nationaal Archief 1.10.29 inv. 5186
scans 62R-63R (margin "No 136. Dat: 1. Fevrier 1757. A: à R:"), transcribed by WAINUT (21 Oct 2026,
`wainut/register_clear.txt`) and checked line by line against the NA IIIF scans 23 Oct 2026 (r2q). The register is the
deciphered text of the same letter: with this folder's key (`wainut/align_register.py`, `wainut/align/R1064.tsv`), 996
of the 1,201 keyed groups stand at the register's word (0.829; shuffled key values 0.153, max 0.180). The cipher is
written in numbered paragraphs set out of order on the five pages (read/R1064.md); the register gives them in order,
which is followed here. The postscript ("Je joins ici deux Lettres ...") is in clear on the last cipher page as well.
The reading follows the register in the clerk's spelling, except where the figures give another word; those places are
listed under "Figures over the register" and in `R1064_reread_log.md`. Groups the register passes over without a word
are listed in the log, not put into the reading. The keyed decode this replaces is `R1064.md`.

## Reading

Les Etats de Hollande se sont separés, et doivent se rassembler le 20. de ce Mois pour traiter l'affaire de
l'augmentation. J'espere que ce sera sans succés pour ceux qui la desirent. Les fonds pour l'Etat de Guerre de cette
année ne sont pas plus forts que ceux de l'année precedente, et les moyens qu'on se propose pour employer pour en
trouver paroitront si onereux, qu'il est aparent que cette seule proposition arretera tout: Ils doivent porter une
Augmentation d'une sixieme sur la Taxe des Maisons, une augmentation generale sur les accises, et une Reduction des
Interêts sur les fonds publics à 2. pour Cent. Il est à presumer que les Etats ne consentiront point à une Operation
qui fouleroit le Peuple, revolteroit les riches, et decrediteroit totalement les fonds publics chez l'Etranger.
J'aurai l'honneur de vous rendre compte de l'Etat de cette affaire dans le tems méme de l'assemblée si je le peux. Je
verrai aujourd'huy l'Italien. J'executerai ce que vous m'ordonnez, et les arrangements que je prendrai avec lui,
seront le sujet de ma premiere Depêche. Il est indubitable que Mr D'Urvoy, qui écrivoit de Londres au Senechal de
Quintin, qui a voyagé D'Anvers à Breda avec le Negociant de Lyon, et dont Mr de Hatzel m'avoit écrit, ne soit le même
homme. Je suis assuré que s'il vient en Hollande, il sera arrêté, et si cela arrive, je ne perdrai pas un moment à
vous en donner avis, et à vous demander les Ordres de Sa Majesté à cet égard. J'attends sur ce qui le regarde une
Reponse de Mr de Hatzel. Par les Lumieres que j'ai cherché à acquerir, Monsr; sur nôtre Commerce, j'ai vû combien il
étoit important de diminuer le benefice de la Pêche Hollandoise du Harang, et d'encourager la nôtre; mais cet
encouragement ne m'a pas paru essentiel dans un tems, ou nous n'avons que trop d'occasion d'employer nos Matelots.
J'ai crû qu'il ne seroit pas d'un grand préjudice aux Sujets de Sa Majesté de voir faciliter une importante Pêche à la
quelle ils ne peuvent fournir tant que la Guerre durera D'ailleurs cette Grace n'est point un Traité, et n'est que
momentanée. Elle auroit encore été le prix de la bonne Conduite, et aroit pu cesser au moment ou le Roy l'auroit crû
convenable au bien de ses Sujets, ou dans l'instant ou Sa Maj: auroit cessé d'être satisfaite de la Republique. Cette
Grace est de toutes celles que le Roy peut accorder à ce Païs la plus desirée, parce qu'elle interesse presque tous
les habitans de la Province de Hollande, et comme cette Pêche est le point de jalousie le plus vif entre l'Angleterre
et la Republique; cette faveur les éloigneroit encore l'une de l'autre. Je ne vous la demanderois cependant Mr. que
conditionellement, et en me mettant à portée de la communiquer si la Hollande resiste à l'Augmentation, et si Elle se
conduit bien, ou de la taire si Elle se laissoit entrainer Je vous prie de vouloir bien dire à Mr. le Marechal de
Belle Isle que Mr. de Nispen, Capitaine dans Lowendal est venú ici, et que je compte vous envoyer Jeudi ou Vendredi un
detail Chiffré de ses observations. L'Italien m'a dit aujourdhuy, qu'un nommé La Combe, qui doit être Commis chez un
Fermier General, étoit parti d'Angleterre il y a deux Ans, qu'il avoit emprunté à son depart de l'Argent de Pichon, et
qu'il en empruntoit encore pour entretenir Correspondance avec lui. Ce Lacombe est le même qui a traduit les Lettres
Philosophiques et Politiques de Mr le Comte d'Orreri. Il m'a dit de plus qu'un nommé Mr Lanzi de Liège, agé d'à peu
près 50. ans, parlant fort bien Italien, François, Anglois, et Latin, est parti d'Anglet: il y a trois Semaines pour
la France, qu'il est chargé de rendre compte de l'etat present de nôtre Marine, et de s'introduire chez quelque Commis
Subalterne de nos Bureaux. Vous verrez Mr dans la fin du Suplement de la Gazette d'Amsterdam d'aujourdhuy un Article
de Paris du 24. . . . Il est vrai que les deux Capitaines, dont il y est parlé, ont passé ici. Voici l'Extrait de mon
Registre des Passeports du 18. Janvier du dit jour. "Les Srs Joseph du Perieux, Capitaine du Navire le Comte de
Maurepas, de Bourdeaux, natif de Libourne, Bernard Clement Omeri, Chirurgien, natif de Coutrai, et Perot Imbert,
Capitaine du Navire la Subtile de Bourdeaux, natif de la dite Ville, allant à Paris, et de là à Bourdeaux, pour un
Mois." Vous remarquerez que le Passeport regardoit les deux Capitaines et le Chirurgien, et que la Gazette ne parle
que des deux Premiers. Je joins ici deux Lettres une pour Mr de Montaran et l'autre pour Mr de Cremille. Vous
trouverez Mr dans ce Paquet trois Exemplaires d'un Ouvrage auquel Mr Rouillé d'Orfeüil a fourni la forme et la
Diction. J'espere que vous en Serez content. [Notes in the register:] La Lettre de N: à Son Oncle van Oller étoit
enfermée dans une Enveloppe addressée à Mr de Montaran. [The work:] La Verité des Faits.

## Figures over the register

- "qui écrivoit de Londres au Senechal de Quintin," for the register's "qui écrivoit de Londres au (-) ne et soit Mr
  de Quintin,": groups 739 1127 134 1136 582 219 1044 580 588 141: au se-ne-cha-l de qui-n-ti-n. The clerk left a
  blank, wrote 'ne' and 'soit', dotted them for deletion and interlined 'et' (62R, checked on the scan): he did not
  make out the title. 1136 = cha also in R1054 'Champ'.
- "ne consentiront point à une Operation" for the register's "ne consentiront pas à une Operation": group 948 = point
  (also 'le point de jalousie' in this letter, 'On ne dit point' in R1054).
- "j'ai vû combien il étoit important" for the register's "j'ai vû combien il est important": group 842 = étoit/été,
  never est.
- "cet encouragement ne" for the register's "cet encouragemen ne": word cut at the binding on 63L; group 308 = ment.
- "cette Grace n'est point un Traité" for the register's "cette Grace n'est pas un Traité": group 948 = point.
- "l'auroit crû" for the register's "l'au roit crû": line break in the register.
- "dans l'instant" for the register's "dans l'in stant": line break in the register.
- "ou de la taire si Elle" for the register's "ou de le taire si Elle": groups 583 172 1017 (la, ta, re); the scan
  (63L) has 'ou de Se taire', not 'le taire' as WAINUT's transcription.
- "Correspondance avec lui." for the register's "Correspondance avec avec lui.": catchword 'avec' at the foot of 63L
  repeated on 63R.
- "du 18. Janvier du dit jour" for the register's "du 10. Janvier du dit jour": the scan (63R) has '18.', not '10.' as
  WAINUT's transcription; the cipher groups (978 634 114) have no value.
- ""Les Srs Joseph du Perieux" for the register's ""Les Srs Joseph " du Perieux": the clerk's quotation marks at each
  line start.
- "Maurepas, de Bourdeaux" for the register's "Maurepas, " de Bourdeaux": the same.
- "Clement Omeri" for the register's "Clement " Omeri": the same.
- "Imbert, Capitaine" for the register's "Imbert, " Capitaine": the same.
- "natif de la dite" for the register's "natif " de la dite": the same.
- "Bourdeaux, pour un Mois."" for the register's "Bourdeaux, " pour un Mois."": the same.
- "Vous remarquerez que le Passeport regardoit les deux Capitaines et le Chirurgien," for the register's "Vous
  remarqué que le Passeport regardoit et le Chirurgien,": groups 1017 718 1116 (re, [718], rés) give 'remarquerez';
  then 282 936 380 265 729 460 172 516 134 797 (re-garde-oit les deux ca-pi-ta[-]ne-s): the figures carry 'les deux
  Capitaines', which the clerk left out (the 'et' after 'regardoit' is dotted on 63R).
- "Montaran et" for the register's "Montaran # et": footnote mark.
- "Ouvrage auquel" for the register's "Ouvrage ## auquel": footnote mark.
- "J'espere que vous en Serez content. [Notes in the register:] La Lettre de N: à Son Oncle van Oller étoit enfermée
  dans une Enveloppe addressée à Mr de Montaran. [The work:] La Verité des Faits." for the register's "J'espere que
  vous en Serez content. dans une Enveloppe addressée à Mr de Montaran.": the clerk's two footnotes (63R), which the
  joiner drops because they begin with '#'.

<!-- English translation of read/R1064_reading.md (Claude Opus 5.5, 23 Oct 2026): the reading follows the contemporary clear copy in the Fagel register, aligned with the keyed decode; where the figures give another word they are followed. -->

# R1064, d'Affry to Rouillé, The Hague, 1 February 1757 (no. 136): English translation

The States of Holland have separated and are to reassemble on the 20th of this month to deal with the matter of the
augmentation. I hope it will be without success for those who want it. The funds for the war establishment this year
are no larger than last year's, and the means that are proposed for finding them will appear so burdensome that it is
likely this proposal alone will stop everything: they are to consist of an increase of one sixth on the house tax, a
general increase of the excises, and a reduction of the interest on the public funds to 2 per cent. It is to be
presumed that the States will not consent to an operation that would crush the people, revolt the rich and wholly
discredit the public funds abroad. I shall have the honour to report to you on the state of this affair during the
assembly itself, if I can. I shall see the Italian today. I shall carry out what you order me, and the arrangements I
make with him will be the subject of my first despatch. There is no doubt that M. d'Urvoy, who was writing from London
to the Seneschal of Quintin, who travelled from Antwerp to Breda with the merchant from Lyon, and about whom M. de
Hatzel had written to me, is the same man. I am sure that if he comes to Holland he will be arrested, and if that
happens I shall not lose a moment in informing you and in asking for His Majesty's orders in the matter. I am waiting
for a reply from M. de Hatzel about what concerns him.

From the knowledge I have tried to acquire, Sir, about our trade, I have seen how important it was to reduce the
profit of the Dutch herring fishery and to encourage our own; but this encouragement did not seem to me essential at a
time when we have only too much occasion to employ our sailors. I thought it would not be a great prejudice to His
Majesty's subjects to see an important fishery made easier, to which they cannot contribute as long as the war lasts.
Besides, this favour is not a treaty, and it is only temporary. It would also have been the price of good conduct, and
could have ceased at the moment the King thought it fitting for the good of his subjects, or at the instant His
Majesty ceased to be satisfied with the Republic. Of all those the King can grant to this country, this favour is the
most desired, because it concerns nearly all the inhabitants of the province of Holland; and as this fishery is the
sharpest point of jealousy between England and the Republic, this favour would set them further apart. I would
nevertheless ask you for it, Sir, only on conditions, and putting myself in a position to communicate it if Holland
resists the augmentation and behaves well, or to keep it quiet if she let herself be carried along.

I beg you to be so good as to tell Marshal de Belle-Isle that M. de Nispen, a captain in Löwendal's regiment, has come
here, and that I expect to send you on Thursday or Friday a ciphered account of his observations. The Italian told me
today that one La Combe, who must be a clerk with a farmer-general, left England two years ago, that on leaving he had
borrowed money from Pichon, and that he was borrowing more of it to keep up a correspondence with him. This Lacombe is
the same who translated the Philosophical and Political Letters of the Earl of Orrery. He also told me that one M.
Lanzi of Liège, about 50 years old, who speaks Italian, French, English and Latin very well, left England three weeks
ago for France, that he is charged with reporting on the present state of our navy and with getting himself in with
some junior clerk of our offices.

You will see, Sir, at the end of the supplement of today's Amsterdam Gazette, an article from Paris of the 24th. It is
true that the two captains it speaks of passed through here. Here is the extract from my register of passports of 18
January, of that day: "Messrs Joseph du Perieux, captain of the ship Comte de Maurepas of Bordeaux, native of
Libourne; Bernard Clement Omeri, surgeon, native of Coutras (written Coutrai); and Perot Imbert, captain of the ship
la Subtile of Bordeaux, native of that town; going to Paris, and from there to Bordeaux, for one month." You will
notice that the passport concerned the two captains and the surgeon, and that the Gazette speaks only of the first two.

I enclose two letters, one for M. de Montaran and the other for M. de Crémille. You will find in this packet, Sir,
three copies of a work to which M. Rouillé d'Orfeuil gave the form and the wording. I hope you will be pleased with it.
[Notes in the register:] The letter of N. to his uncle van Oller was enclosed in an envelope addressed to M. de
Montaran. [The work:] La Vérité des Faits.

R1065, 3 Feb 1757, no. 137

Lyonet's contemporary clear decipherment, imaged on the DECODE record and transcribed here.

Decryption

R1065.txt

P1: J'ai eu l'honneur de vous marquer avanthier que les Etats de Hollande se rassembleroient le 8 de ce Mois. Cela m'a paru si singulier, que j'ai voulu m'en éclaircir, et j'ai été en droiture hier au soir chez le Conseiller Pensionaire, il m'a dit qu'il se pourroit que l'Assemblée reprit effectivement dans ce tems
P2: là, et qu'il comptoit cependant la pouvoir retarder jusqu'au jour de jeûne. Je lui ai repeté à l'egard de l'augmentation ce que je lui ai encore dit. Il me paroit ne pas s'éloigner d'en faire un objet. A la verité ce sentiment part de la peur reelle qu'il a de perdre sa Place; mais comme tous les
P3: Republicains veulent qu'il la conserve, je ne serois point étonné de le voir travailler foiblement à l'Augmentation, avec la certitude de la voir traverser par Eux. Il m'a dit encore que si l'on s'y determinoit ce ne seroit pas sans prendre la precaution de declarer aux autres Puissances voisines quel est l'objet de
P4: cette demarche. Je lui ai repondu: vous devez bien juger, Mr, que le Roi mon Maitre n'aprendroit pas la nouvelle d'une demarche qui pourroit être contraire à la neutralité que vous avez promise, sans m'ordonner de vous faire des questions très positives sur les intentions de la Republique. J'espère en savoir d'avantage avant le bout de ce Mois. J'aurai l'honneur de vous
P5: en rendre compte. Mr de Nispen, qui est venu ici, en est parti ce matin pour executer ce que Mr de Cremille lui a ordonné. Il sera dans 6 ou 7 jours à Maseik, et je suis certain qu'il executera bien tout ce qui lui est prescrit; mais je vous suplie de vouloir bien representer à Mr le Marechal de Belle
P6: Ille, combien il est incommode d'être reduit pour entrer dans le Pais ennemi à des routes assez detournées pour ne pouvoir cheminer que par des Divisions de deux Bataillons ou de quatre Bataillons. Je conviens que la Gueldre est si depourvue de Troupes qu'on ne doit pas craindre d'en trouver en campagne ni sur son
P7: chemin; mais cette facon d'arriver successive à l'inconvenient d'annoncer une Armée de bien loin; moyennant quoi les subsistances que ce Pais pourroit nous fournir seront en partie renfermées dans Wesel, et dans Gueldres; le reste se sauvera dans la Gueldre hollandoise avec d'autant plus de facilité que le
P8: Rhin et la Meuse y conduisent. Je croirois donc qu'il seroit très utile de faire preceder l'Avant garde de l'Armée par un corps considerable de Troupes legeres, et donner à ce corps quelques Pièces de Campagne les plus legères: aussi ce corps pourroit se porter avec vivacité du coté de Clèves et sur le Rhin
P9: et empêcheroit par là l'exportation d'une grande partie des subsistances. Mr de Nispen s'est chargé de rendre compte à Mr de Cremille de l'utilité et même de la necessité de cet arrangement, au moyen de quoi je n'en ferai point de Memoire separé. Le Courier de Mr de Reischach a profité d'un moment où le vent s'est trouvé favorable le 26 de ce Mois dernier, et quoi que le
P10: vent ait varié, j'espère qu'il sera arrivé en Angleterre et que vous pourrez dans peu en avoir des nouvelles. J'ai donné à l'Italien les Cent Louis suivant les ordres de Sa Majesté. Cet homme est parti ce matin pour se rendre à Helvoet et de là en Angleterre. Il m'assure qu'il viendroit à bout de conduire Pichon à Liège. Il se servira du pretexte d'examiner nos Demarches, et de donner
P11: avis à Hanover de tout ce que nous ferons. Comme il arrive souvent qu'on ne peut pas passer ici par les vents d'Est et de Nord Est, et que ces vents n'empêchent pas d'arriver à Ostende, il m'écrira par cette voye en droiture. J'aviserai Mr d'Aubigni [cipher adds: à Liège] en même temps que j'aurai l'honneur de vous faire part des nouvelles de
P12: l'Italien si vous le trouvez bon. Je vous ai envoyé avanthier une Note sur un nommé Lacombe. Je crois qu'il faudroit ne faire aucune poursuite sur ce qui le regarde avant d'avoir Pichon, parce que celui ci pourroit s'effaroucher s'il aprenoit que Lacombe a disparu, et il est à presumer que Pichon a plus d'un correspondant. L'Ambassadeur d'Espagne a depêché
P13: hier un courrier pour Paris. Je ne sais à quel sujet.
Translation

R1065_en.md

<!-- English translation of plain/R1065.txt (Claude Opus 5.5, 23 Oct 2026): Lyonet's contemporary clear decipherment of d'Affry's no. 137, 3 Feb 1757, imaged on the DECODE record and transcribed here. The P1-P13 piece numbers of the copy are kept; the pieces run on mid-sentence. -->

# R1065, d'Affry to Rouillé, The Hague, 3 February 1757 (no. 137): English translation of the contemporary decipherment

P1: I had the honour to tell you the day before yesterday that the States of Holland would reassemble on the 8th of
this month. This seemed so odd to me that I wished to clear it up, and I went straight to the Grand Pensionary's
yesterday evening. He told me that the Assembly might indeed resume at that time,

P2: and that he nevertheless expected to be able to delay it until the fast day. I repeated to him, regarding the
augmentation, what I had told him before. He does not seem to me far from making an issue of it. In truth this
sentiment comes from his real fear of losing his post; but as all the

P3: Republicans want him to keep it, I would not be surprised to see him work feebly for the augmentation, certain of
seeing it thwarted by them. He also told me that if they decided on it, it would not be without taking the precaution
of declaring to the other neighbouring powers what the purpose of

P4: this step is. I answered him: you must surely judge, Sir, that the King my master would not learn the news of a step
that might be contrary to the neutrality you have promised without ordering me to put very positive questions to you
about the Republic's intentions. I hope to know more before the end of this month. I shall have the honour to

P5: report to you on it. Mr de Nispen, who came here, left this morning to carry out what Mr de Crémille ordered him.
He will be at Maaseik in 6 or 7 days, and I am certain that he will carry out well everything prescribed to him; but I
beg you to be so good as to represent to Marshal de Belle-

P6: Isle how inconvenient it is, in entering enemy country, to be reduced to roads so out of the way that one can only
advance in divisions of two or four battalions. I agree that Guelders is so bare of troops that one need not fear to
find any in the field or on one's

P7: way; but this manner of arriving piecemeal has the drawback of announcing an army from very far off; by which
means the provisions that this country could furnish us will be partly shut up in Wesel and in Guelders, and the rest
will be saved into Dutch Guelders, all the more easily since the

P8: Rhine and the Meuse lead there. I would therefore think it very useful to send ahead of the army's advance guard a
considerable corps of light troops, and to give this corps a few of the lightest field pieces: this corps could then
move briskly towards Cleves and onto the Rhine

P9: and would thereby prevent the export of a great part of the provisions. Mr de Nispen has undertaken to explain to
Mr de Crémille the usefulness and even the necessity of this arrangement, so that I shall not make a separate memorandum
of it. Mr von Reischach's courier took advantage of a moment when the wind was favourable on the 26th of last month,
and although the

P10: wind has varied, I hope he will have arrived in England and that you will soon have news of him. I have given the
Italian the hundred louis according to His Majesty's orders. This man left this morning for Helvoet and from there for
England. He assures me that he would manage to bring Pichon to Liège. He will use the pretext of watching our moves and
of reporting

P11: to Hanover everything we do. As it often happens that one cannot cross here with east and north-east winds, and
these winds do not prevent arriving at Ostend, he will write to me directly by that route. I shall inform Mr d'Aubigni
[the cipher adds: at Liège] at the same time as I have the honour to pass on to you the news of

P12: the Italian, if you approve. The day before yesterday I sent you a note on a certain Lacombe. I think no action
should be taken concerning him before we have Pichon, because the latter might take fright if he learned that Lacombe
had disappeared, and it is to be presumed that Pichon has more than one correspondent. The Spanish ambassador
despatched

P13: a courier to Paris yesterday. I do not know on what business.

Decryption

R1066.txt

J'ai vu Mr de Slingerland chez moi ce matin. Je lui ai dit ce que vous me prescrivez par votre depêche du 11e à l'egard de la negociation avec l'Angleterre. Il m'a repondu qu'il croyoit devoir me communiquer la substance de la lettre de Mr de Holdernesse à Mr Yorke. La voici telle qu'il me l'a dictée. Sa Majesté Britannique a evité la guerre autant que la prudence et les interets de sa part l'ont permis. Sa Majesté desire sincerement la paix, pourvu que les conditions en soient assez equitables pour la rendre durable, et qu'elle comprenne touts les alliés respectifs, et qu'elle conserve les droits et les possessions des sujets de la Grande Bretagne. En ce cas, le Roi d'Angleterre verra avec plaisir cette negociation entamée entre Mr Yorke et Mr D'Affry, sans entremise d'un tiers. Vous voyez, Mr, que ce langage n'a rien d'excessif; mais qu'il n'a rien non plus de recevable, puis qu'il est juste que l'Angleterre fasse les premiers pas. D'ailleurs la reserve de touts ses alliés, et la condition vague des droits et possessions, n'offre pas des conditions aisées.
Translation

R1066_en.md

<!-- English translation of plain/R1066.txt (Claude Opus 5.5, 23 Oct 2026): Lyonet's contemporary clear decipherment, imaged on the DECODE record R1066 and transcribed here. -->

# R1066, d'Affry, The Hague, 1757: English translation of the contemporary decipherment

I saw Mr van Slingelandt at my house this morning. I told him what you prescribe to me in your despatch of the 11th
regarding the negotiation with England. He answered that he thought he should communicate to me the substance of
Lord Holderness's letter to Mr Yorke. Here it is as he dictated it to me. His Britannic Majesty has avoided war as far
as prudence and the interests on his side allowed. His Majesty sincerely desires peace, provided that its conditions
are equitable enough to make it lasting, that it includes all the respective allies, and that it preserves the rights
and possessions of the subjects of Great Britain. In that case the King of England will be pleased to see this
negotiation opened between Mr Yorke and Mr d'Affry, without the mediation of a third party. You see, Sir, that this
language has nothing excessive in it; but neither has it anything acceptable, since it is right that England should
take the first steps. Besides, the reservation of all its allies, and the vague condition of rights and possessions,
do not offer easy terms.

R1067, 1 Apr 1757, no. 161

D'Affry reports his interview with Pensionary Steyn on the King's orders (the demand for an answer on the passage of French troops by Maastricht/Namur): Steyn, embarrassed and under attack by enemies trying to bring him down, could not answer before the week-end. D'Affry then saw the Gouvernante (Anne of Hanover), gave her the King's declaration; she answered "must it be I who favour the means of doing harm to my father?" (George II); he asked her to use her credit to obtain the answer the King had a right to expect.

Transcription

# R1067 - d'Affry to Rouillé, The Hague, 1 April 1757

No. 161 ("D'aff à R", "No 161.", "Dat: Premier Avril 1757" in clear at the head of IMG_R1067_I5580_P2). Six pages
(I5579..I5584), about 1480 code groups: the ciphered periods of a much longer letter, the clear text not transcribed.
Read with key_M through a parser of the DOC transcription (group values from sibling `read/*_new.tsv` added in braces);
no image crops made in this pass.

## Order of the pieces

The pieces carry labels "No 2" ... "No 13" (no "No 1" label: the unlabelled opening under the header is No 1; no "No 8"
appears in the DOC). **The numbers do not give the reading order.** Read in numerical order the text breaks at every
join (No 1 ends "... tu re", No 2 begins "je [n] ai parlé ..."). Read in page order the joins make sense:

    P2 (I5580)  No 1  ->  P3 (I5581) No 1 end, No 3, No 5
    P4 (I5582)  No 2, No 4, No 6  ->  P5 (I5583) No 6 end, No 10, No 12  ->  P6 (I5584) No 12 end, No 7, No 9
    P1 (I5579)  No 11, No 13 (+ vertical margin line)

Joins that check: No 1 "... [elle me dit] tu" + No 3 "es pas ministre..." ; No 2 "ne sois" + No 4 "honoré" ;
No 6 "portée tout de suite aux États Généraux" + No 10 "avant que ces bataillons..." ; No 10 "un avis ... de" + No 12
"l'Ostfrise". The numbers are probably keyed to places in the clear draft, not the order of the cipher. Image I5579
(P1) carries the header-less No 11/No 13 and reads as the end of the letter (the scan order is off by one page).
